A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold”, “legend” or “named”).

A p-value with the name of a correction or a test after the p (“pFWE < .05”, “padj < 0.05”, “p Wilcoxon = .02”) is flagged only when a test statistic comes before it in its clause (“t(37) = 7.17, p FWE < .05”). Without one it is mostly the rule that decided what was significant (“genes with padj < 0.05 were selected”); such a p-value is “named” in not_a_test. Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

A p-value below 0 or above 1 is impossible; it is listed apart and not also as imprecise. Often the statistic before it, or its minus sign, was copied into it (“t(134) = -2.32, p = -.13”, “Z = 3.091, P = 3.091”). A value above 1 is listed only after a test statistic in its clause: without one, a “P” above 1 was nearly always another quantity (a proportion, pH, a group’s mean, a model parameter). A number in scientific notation whose power of ten was lost when the PDF was read (“p = 2.4 x 10 -9” read as 2.4) is not listed. A “p” that is the subscript of another symbol (“r p = .64”, “²p = .02” for partial eta squared, “Δp = .33”), or a quantity with a unit, a logarithm or a ratio (“P = 2.9 µm”, “-log10 P = 7.5”, “Ca/P = 1.67”), is not read as a p-value at all (see extract_p_values()). Each p-value has its own row, also when the same one is written twice in a sentence.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: Validated on 226 Psychological Science papers with 5,019 hand-coded p-values, 438 of them imprecise (scienceverse/validation, branch stat_p_exact). Version 0.4.4.3 reported 419 p-values as imprecise, 388 of them correctly (93%), and missed 50 (89% found). Of the misses, 13 are starred table notes (“*p < .05”) that the module leaves out on purpose and 11 are p-values it did not find; of the wrong reports, 20 are p-values the ground truth does not contain (such as “ps < .05”) and 6 use ≤ or ≥. These figures measure agreement with the hand-coding, which has known errors in both directions (scienceverse/validation#48).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

A p-value in a table note (“† p < .10, * p < .05”) or a figure legend (“dashed lines indicate non-significant paths (p > .05)”) is left out, as in stat_p_exact; the column not_a_test says why. A p-value next to words that state a threshold is kept, because such sentences often report a result against that level (“not significant at the conventional threshold (p = .054)”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: Validated on three hand-coded sets of papers (scienceverse/validation: 249 Psychological Science, 250 Economics and 250 MDPI papers). Of 1,783 non-significant p-values in the ground truth, version 0.4.4.3 found 1,719 (3.6% missed), and 94.8% of its 1,814 detections were in the ground truth. Some differences come from the ground truth: it lacks p-values with a correction name (“p Bonf = .061”), and counts partial eta squared written with its subscript after it (“η 2 p = .05”), “p = .05”, and some table notes and figure legends (“† p < .10, *p < .05”), which this module leaves out, as non-significant p-values. These figures measure agreement with the hand-coding, which has known errors in both directions (scienceverse/validation#48).

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

A link to a file-sharing service (Google Drive, Dropbox, OneDrive, Box, WeTransfer, MEGA, iCloud Drive, Baidu Netdisk, SURFdrive; see [file_share_links()]) gets a warning and makes the light yellow: such services are not repositories, as files can be changed or deleted at any time, get no DOI, and the link stops working when the account is closed. The report advises a stable repository such as Zenodo instead. Their files are not read, and summary_table$file_share_n counts the links.

Deposits in archives for sequence and expression data (GEO, the INSDC archives BioProject, SRA, ENA and DDBJ, and ArrayExpress; see [seq_archive_links()]), mostly named by accession number, are read for whose deposit they are. The authors’ own are listed with what they hold ([seq_archive_size()]) and are not downloaded or checked as data and code; summary_table$archive_own_n counts them.

R packages on CRAN ([cran_links()], [cran_info()]) are the paper’s own when a paper author is a package author and the paper presents the package; the paper’s own are listed with their version and not checked (summary_table$cran_own_n). A Handle link (hdl.handle.net/...) that leads to a repository read here is checked as a link to that repository ([handle_links()]).

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

Whose deposit is each link? The ownership table gives, for every repository linked in the text and (with references = TRUE) every deposit cited in the reference list, one of six labels: own_this (made by the authors for this paper), own_earlier_data or own_earlier_code (the authors’ own, from earlier work), others_data or others_code (made by other people, reused here), or not_data (a publication such as a preprint, not a deposit), with ownership_group (“own”, “others”, “not_data”) and the reason. It is decided without a language model, from the sentence or reference that cites the link, the paper’s authors (names, ORCID iDs, affiliations), and what the deposit’s own record and files say (who made it, what it names, when, whether it is a software package; for an OSF project cited only in the reference list, its record alone). On a round of labelled links the rules had not been written from, the three groups were right for 98% of links in the main text (93 of 95) and 90% in reference lists (74 of 82); the labels were made by Claude agents from each link’s evidence, and disputed ones checked against the deposits. Telling this paper’s own deposit from the authors’ earlier one is less certain.

Only the deposits that are this paper’s own are checked. A deposit linked in the text that holds other people’s work (others_data, others_code) or the authors’ earlier work (own_earlier_data, own_earlier_code) is listed in the report, with the reason, but its files are left out of table and kept in table_not_checked: they count toward nothing here (files that do not belong in a repository, naming, READMEs, studies, the traffic light), and code_check, data_check and reproducibility_check, which read table, do not check them either. reproducibility_check still puts the files of theirs that the paper’s code names in the folder the code runs in, as a re-analysis needs the data it re-analyses. When every linked deposit is set aside, the traffic light is “na”. A deposit that is the paper’s own is counted as such when its README, title or description names the paper (its title or DOI).

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability).

Provenance, kept so that a TRACE Research Object (TRO) declaration of the run can be written later from a stored result (metacheck #404; the helpers are in R/provenance.R). Only when code was run (execute = TRUE): * run_results$started_at, run_results$ended_at: when each run of a script started and ended (ISO 8601, UTC, milliseconds); NA for a script that was not started. A script run again in a later pass has the times of its last run. A TRO uses them for each trov:TransparentResearchPerformance. * run_results$exit_status: the exit status of the process that ran the script (0 = ended normally); NA when the process did not report one (a time limit, or Docker failing to start the container). * artifacts: one row per file, with stage (deposit: as downloaded from the repository, hashed from the downloaded file; before_run: the run folder just before the first script ran, after metacheck’s own changes, such as rewritten paths and unpacked archives; after_run: only the files the scripts made (status new), changed (changed) or removed (removed), as an unchanged file is the one listed before the run), path (relative to the run folder, as valid UTF-8, a byte of another encoding written as <xx>), size (bytes), sha256, mime_type (by extension, NA when unknown), source (the file’s address in the repository, for deposit). The package libraries metacheck installs in the run folder are left out, as are metacheck’s own files there (a script’s runner, and the statistical output it writes in statistical_output/). Files metacheck itself copied or made between passes (see modifications, output_copied, file_placed) show in after_run too. A TRO uses the rows as trov:ResearchArtifacts and each stage as a trov:ArtifactArrangement. * modifications$sha256_before, modifications$sha256_after: the hash of the changed script as downloaded and as it ran; NA when the script is not at the same path in both (a script made from an R Markdown file). * environment: the system that ran the code (trov:TransparentResearchSystem in a TRO): metacheck_version, started_at, ended_at, host (R version, platform, operating system), sandbox, network_isolated (TRUE only when every Docker run had --network none; the process sandbox does not stop the network), internet, timeout_per_script, total_timeout, python_local (the Python run outside Docker and its version), and docker: runs (for each kind of run, r_run, r_install, python_run, python_install, the image and the docker run arguments, such as --network none, --read-only, --cap-drop ALL, no-new-privileges, the process limit and the memory and processor limits) and images (for each image, its ID and digests from docker image inspect, its creation time and the R and Python versions its environment declares).

run_results$checked says what the run of each script shows about the code, in five groups, so that a script that does not reproduce is kept apart from one the check could not test: ran (it ran to the end); code_error (it did not reproduce: it stopped with an error of the code or the deposit, could not be read, or needs a file the deposit does not hold); dependency (a package or program was not available: a package that could not be installed or was not loaded, a program outside R and Python, a system library, or R crashing in a package’s compiled code); time (it did not finish within the time limit for one script, or was not started because the time for all scripts had run out); and not_checkable (it could not be checked automatically: it needs a person, the internet, a key or another setting of the authors’ computer, one platform such as Google Colab, Windows or OpenSAFELY, more memory than the run allows, a file that was not downloaded, a screen, or a program that runs it, or it is a file of a package of the code that Python runs only as a module of that package). It is NA for files run inside another script, files that are not an analysis, files not read, and a Docker failure. summary_table counts each group (repro_ran_n, repro_code_error_n, repro_dependency_n, repro_time_n, repro_not_checkable_n), and the report’s first sentence gives them. The traffic light reflects the static readiness signals by default; a script in code_error that stopped with an error makes it red, as a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure, and a script in one of the other three groups keeps it from being green. Calls that need a screen, which the run does not have (dev.print(), dev.copy2pdf(), dev.copy2eps(), savePlot(), windows(), win.graph(), x11(), quartz(), View()), are replaced before the run by calls that need none, which modifications records as screen_call_replaced. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

33 after HCT beyond baseline titers. 34 • A low cellular composition score identified recipients unlikely to respond to vaccination, in 35 children and adults alike.

36 2.

1 Introduction

Allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation (HCT) is a curative therapy for hematologic malignancies and bone marrow failure syndromes, but recipients remain at substantially elevated risk of severe infection for months to years after transplantation because of the slow, incomplete, and variable recovery of adaptive immunity. [1] [2] [3] [4] [5] Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for all HCT recipients, yet serologic responses are highly variable and a meaningful fraction of recipients fail to mount a fourfold rise in hemagglutination inhibition (HAI) titer to any included antigen. [6] [7] [8] [9] [10] [11] [12] The transplant physician facing a recipient at a routine clinical encounter has no pre-vaccination tool to identify recipients at greatest risk of a weak antibody response, and therefore no rational basis on which to time revaccination, prioritize booster doses, or recommend high-dose formulations in those most likely to benefit. 13, 14 Immune reconstitution after transplantation shapes both susceptibility to infection and the strength of the vaccine response. 15 The clinical correlates of a poor response are known in aggregate (short time since transplantation, active graft-versus-host disease, ongoing immunosuppression [16] [17] [18] [19] [20] ), but they explain only part of the between-recipient variability and do not yield a usable prevaccination tool. We hypothesized that the capacity to mount a serologic vaccine response is reflected in part in the composition of the peripheral lymphoid and myeloid compartments at the time of vaccination, and that this composition carries information that clinical factors alone do not.

Direct measurement of that composition has become feasible with single-cell techniques. In the general population, systems-vaccinology studies have shown that the pre-vaccination immune state predicts the antibody response to influenza and other vaccines, [21] [22] [23] [24] [25] [26] but this baseline-state framework has not been established after allogeneic transplantation. In pediatric HCT recipients enrolled in a multi-center vaccine trial, Amarin and colleagues used mass cytometry to show that the pre-vaccination frequencies of selected B and CD4 + T cell subpopulations are associated with the subsequent influenza HAI response. 27 That analysis was a single-cohort study of univariate associations, and an association is not yet a prediction: a population that differs between responders and non-responders may still add nothing once the pre-vaccination titer is known.

We therefore asked whether the whole pre-vaccination cellular composition predicts the antibody response in recipients the model has never seen, and whether it does so beyond baseline titers and the clinical factors already available at the visit. We studied the first vaccination season of 260 pediatric and adult recipients from 2 multicenter phase 2 trials, profiled by 30-marker mass cytometry before vaccination, and summarized the models as one cellular composition score per recipient, so that the gain could be read as a difference in antibody response. We then asked whether that score identifies recipients unlikely to respond, whether children and adults share the same cellular profile, whether the score predicts response a season later in the same recipients, and whether fewer populations and markers carry the same information.

2 Methods

2.1 Study population

We analyzed the first vaccination season of 260 allogeneic HCT recipients in 2 parallel randomized trials, 1 pediatric (n = 147) and 1 adult (n = 113). Both trials compared high-dose with standard-dose inactivated influenza vaccine in a 2-dose regimen, [28] [29] [30] [31] and their primary outcomes are published. [9] [10] [11] A vaccination episode is one recipient's course in one study year: a pre-vaccination sample at visit V1, the day of vaccination, paired with the titer at V3, 4 weeks after the second dose.

The 74 episodes contributed by recipients who re-enrolled the following year were held out and used only for validation (Section 2.7). Episodes without a recorded V1 or V3 titer were excluded (2 first-season and 7 second-season episodes). Pre-vaccination blood was profiled by 30-marker mass cytometry 32, 33 with the panel of Amarin et al., 27 yielding about 9.9 million first-season cells, a median of about 40,000 per recipient. Marker intensities were arcsinh-transformed (cofactor 5) and corrected for acquisition batch (58 run dates, both cohorts together) with Harmony 34 before analysis. Both trials were approved by the institutional review boards of the participating centers, and all participants or their guardians gave written informed consent. A.A. and S.P. analyzed the data, and all authors had access to the primary trial data.

2.2 Outcome

The response to each antigen was the log fold-rise in hemagglutination inhibition (HAI) titer from V1 to V3, for H1N1, H3N2 and B/Victoria. Each titer was the geometric mean of the duplicate measurements recorded in the trial databases. Samples below the assay detection limit (<1:10) were assigned a titer of 1:5 and samples above the ceiling the ceiling value. B/Yamagata was excluded because it is not in the high-dose formulation for adults.

The primary outcome was the composite response, the mean of the 3 antigen log fold-rises, whose exponential is the geometric mean fold-rise across antigens. One outcome per recipient answers the clinical question, whether this recipient will respond to this vaccination, with the information in all 3 antigens and without testing 3 correlated endpoints separately. A fourfold composite rise is a geometric mean fold-rise of at least 4, which a recipient can meet with a large rise to 1 or 2 antigens and not the third. A fourfold rise is a serologic response criterion and does not directly measure protection against clinical influenza. 35, 36 Models for the 3 antigens separately are in supplemental Section S2.5.

2.3 Prediction models

Four models differ only in their predictor blocks, so their differences isolate what each block adds: M1, the log pre-vaccination titer of each of the 3 antigens; M2, M1 plus vaccine dose, age at consent, sex and months since transplantation; M3, M1 plus the cellular-composition features; and M4, all of them. Each model was an elastic-net penalized linear regression (α = 0.9) of the composite response, with the penalty chosen by cross-validation inside each training fold. Using one penalized model family for all 4 models keeps the penalty from being confounded with the added predictors; fitting M1 and M2 by ordinary least squares instead changed their error by less than 0.2%. Supplemental Methods explains why the pre-vaccination titer can be a predictor of a fold-rise that contains it.

2.4 Cellular composition features

Thirty markers allow cells to be grouped at many levels of detail, and the level that best predicts the antibody response is not known in advance. We therefore treated the clustering resolution as a tuning parameter and let predictive performance choose it.

Within each outer training fold, k-means grouped one million cells into 5,000 micro-clusters and Leiden community detection grouped the micro-cluster centers into the cell populations used as features. Training and test cells were assigned to the nearest micro-cluster and inherited its population label, so a held-out recipient's cells influenced neither the populations nor the model. Each recipient's population fractions entered the models as centered log-ratios, which express each fraction relative to the geometric mean of all fractions in that sample. The resolution, and with it the number of populations, was chosen by 3-fold recipient-grouped cross-validation inside the training fold, by the error of M4 for the composite response relative to an unpenalized titers-plus-clinical model. The selected numbers of populations were 42, 35, 42, 37 and 25 in the 5 folds, and prediction changed modestly across the grid (supplemental Methods; supplemental Figure S1).

For interpretation we also built one shared atlas of 35 populations from the cells of both cohorts and annotated it from marker profiles. The shared atlas is used only to name and compare populations, never to fit the primary models.

2.5 Cellular composition score

The cellular composition score is one number per recipient: the part of the predicted composite log fold-rise that comes from immune-cell composition. It is the sum of the recipient's population features, each multiplied by its coefficient in the M3 model fitted without that recipient, excluding the intercept and the baseline-titer terms. The score is high when a recipient's blood holds relatively more of the populations that accompanied strong responses in other recipients and relatively less of those that accompanied weak ones. Because each fold has its own atlas, scores were standardized within fold (mean 0, standard deviation [SD] 1) before pooling; 1 SD corresponded to a 1.9-fold difference in predicted fold-rise. Standardized scores ranged from -2.8 to 2.1 (5th to 95th percentile, -1.7 to 1.5).

2.6 Statistical analysis

Performance was evaluated by 5-fold outer cross-validation with folds grouped by recipient, and the mean squared error (MSE) averaged across folds, weighted by the number of test recipients. Model comparisons used a recipient-level bootstrap of the out-of-fold prediction error (B = 2,000, fixed seed), with the 95% CI the 2.5th to 97.5th percentile and P two-sided. We summarized the score in fifths, estimated its association with a fourfold composite rise by logistic regression adjusted for baseline titer (odds ratio per SD) and with the observed composite fold-rise by Spearman correlation, and compared the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of the titers-only and titers-plus-composition models for a fourfold composite rise. The P values for these score statistics come from a null bootstrap that repeats resolution selection, penalty tuning and coefficient fitting in each of 999 replicates generated under no cellular effect beyond baseline titers, so they account for the whole fitting pipeline; conventional Wald and DeLong tests are reported beside them (supplemental Table S1). All P values are 2-sided. Analyses used R 4.6.1 with glmnet 37 and igraph; 38 the analysis scripts accompany the study. Mechanics of the atlas, the bootstrap and the null bootstrap are in supplemental Methods.

2.7 Second-season validation

Each second-season episode was assigned to the cross-validation fold of its recipient, mapped to that fold's atlas and scored with that fold's models, which were fitted without the recipient.

Neither season of a recipient contributed to the model that scored it. In these episodes we compared the prediction error and AUC of the titers-only and titers-plus-composition models and, among recipients with both seasons, the change in score and in baseline titer by response in each season.

We also compared the response 4 weeks after the first dose between seasons. The same pipeline run on all 334 episodes with complete titers, with both seasons of a returning recipient in the same fold, is a sensitivity analysis (supplemental Section S3.7).

3 Results

Results 185

3.1 Recipient characteristics 186

The 260 recipients had a median age of 16.6 years (range, 3.1-72.8), 147 in the pediatric trial 187 (median, 11.8 years) and 113 in the adult trial (median, 57.9 years), and were vaccinated a median

3.2 The shared atlas resolved 35 immune-cell populations 193

To give the cellular predictors a biological interpretation, we constructed a shared 35-population 194 atlas using cells from both cohorts and annotated its populations from marker profiles (supplemental 195 Table S2). This provides common cell labels; prediction in the primary analysis used atlases S2 and S3).

Memory T-cell subsets were named from CD45RO and CCR7; because the panel has no CD45RA, effector-memory cells re-expressing CD45RA (EMRA) were identified as CD45RO -CCR7 -. No B-cell population had the CD27 + phenotype of memory B cells. We labeled populations activated when they expressed activation markers (CD69, CD38, OX40 or CD137) above other populations of the same lineage, and cycling when they expressed Ki-67. For example, the double-negative T cells of cluster 32 had the highest CD38 and TIGIT expression of any T-cell population (supplemental Figure S3).

3.3 Adding immune-cell composition improved prediction beyond baseline titers

The four-model comparison isolates the added predictive value of immune-cell composition. Model accuracy is reported as mean squared error (MSE), the average squared difference between the predicted and observed composite log fold-rise; a percentage improvement is the relative reduction in that error, not a proportion of recipients.

Adding immune-cell composition to baseline titers (M1→M3) reduced MSE from 2.277 to 1.929, an improvement of 15.3% (95% recipient-bootstrap CI, 7.0-23.2%; P < .001; Table 2, Figure 2). Clinical factors alone reduced MSE by 6.0% (M1→M2; P = .072), and adding immune-cell composition on top of them still improved prediction by 12.5% (M2→M4; 4.6-20.3%; P = .001). Adding clinical factors to a model that already had cellular composition gave 3.0% (P = .20), and the two information sources together improved prediction by 17.8% over titers alone (M1→M4; 8.7-26.5%; P = .001). Cellular composition therefore carried information that the clinical factors did not, while the reverse was less clear.

Models fitted to the 3 antigens separately gave the same picture with smaller intervals: composition improved prediction by 11.2% beyond titers (4.6-17.6%; P = .003) and by 8.6% beyond titers plus clinical factors (P = .006), with per-antigen gains of 8.7% for H1N1, 12.4% for H3N2 and 227 12.2% for B/Victoria (supplemental Section S2.5, Table S3). Cluster 29 carries CD38, CD138 and CXCR3 without clear B-or T-cell lineage markers, so we could not assign it a cell type. On the shared atlas, removing the four lineage-negative populations that lack lineage markers (clusters 3, 19, 24 and 29) and recomputing composition from the remaining 31 did not reduce the gain beyond baseline titers (18.6% versus 18.1% with all 35 shared-atlas populations; supplemental Section S2.6), so the prediction does not depend on these unassigned cells.

3.4 B-cell, T-cell and monocyte populations drove the prediction

3.5 Prediction gains were present in every baseline-titer quartile

A high pre-vaccination titer has been associated with a smaller fold-rise after vaccination in several studies, 22,39 and recipients with low baseline titers are the ones at most risk, so we asked where the gain came from across the baseline range of HAI titers.

Immune-cell composition improved prediction in all four baseline-titer quartiles, by 18.3% in the lowest, 14.9% and 12.5% in the middle two and 7.9% in the highest (Table 3). The gain was largest where it matters most, among recipients whose pre-vaccination titers leave the most room to respond, and it fell steadily as baseline titers rose.

3.6 A low cellular composition score identified recipients unlikely to respond

A reduction in squared error is hard to act on at the bedside, so we summarized the same models as one number per recipient: the cellular composition score, the part of the predicted response that comes from immune-cell composition (Section 2.5). Standardized scores ranged from -2.8 to 2.1 SD. A low score corresponds to a recognizable blood composition (Figure 4A). Compared with recipients in the highest fifth of the score, those in the lowest fifth had a median B-cell share about about one-eighth as large (3.8% versus 28.9% of PBMCs, with plasmablasts counted as B cells) and about half the share of CD4 + T cells, but about 3.8 times the share of monocytes and other antigen-presenting cells and 2.4 times the share of lineage-negative cells (supplemental Figure S8).

Fourfold composite rises became more frequent from the lowest to the highest fifth of the score, from 15% (8 of 52 recipients) to 67% (35 of 52; Figure 4B). Outside the lowest fifth, 51% of recipients had a fourfold rise (difference, 36 percentage points; 95% CI, 23-47; P = .001). At a given baseline titer, each SD of the score was associated with 2.2-fold higher odds of a fourfold rise (95% CI, 1.6-3.0; P = .007) and a 1.9-fold larger geometric mean fold-rise (1.6-2.2). Figure 4C shows this across the range of baseline titers: at any baseline titer, recipients with lower scores were less likely to respond. Among recipients with a pre-vaccination HAI titer below 1:40, 24% to 50% of those in the lowest fifth reached 1:40, depending on antigen, against 71% to 87% of the others (supplemental Figure S9B).

Adding immune-cell composition to baseline titers raised the AUC for a fourfold composite rise from 0.75 to 0.80 (difference, 0.050; 95% CI, 0.013-0.089; training-aware P ≤ .001, DeLong P = .009; Figure 4D), and adding it to titers and clinical factors raised the AUC from 0.78 to 0.81 (P = .034). Immune-cell composition was associated with time since transplantation (supplemental Section S3.4), but the score added information beyond it: adjusted for months since transplantation, vaccine dose, age and cohort, the odds ratio per SD was unchanged (2.1; 95% CI, 1.5-3.0; supplemental Figure S9A). The Spearman rank correlation between the score and the observed composite fold-rise was 0.39 (P ≤ .001), and the same gradient appeared for each antigen separately (supplemental Figures S10, S11 and S12; supplemental Table S4). When recipients in each of the lower three baseline-titer quartiles, where a fourfold rise is attainable, were split at that quartile's median score and pooled, those above the median had a 2.7-fold larger geometric mean composite fold-rise than those at or below it (9.9 versus 3.7; supplemental Section S2.9, Figure S13 and Table S5). At the same score, responders differed from non-responders mainly in a lower baseline titer and a longer interval since transplantation (supplemental Figure S14). Responses overlapped across score levels, so the score separates groups more clearly than it separates individual recipients.

3.7 Children and adults shared the cellular profile

Pediatric and adult recipients differ in age, conditioning and immune recovery, so pooling them has to be justified. We evaluated the pooled model within each cohort and refitted the models separately in each cohort (Figure 5A). The pooled model reduced prediction error beyond baseline titers by 9.4% in children (P = .15) and 22.9% in adults (P < .001), so the pooled model improved prediction in both, more in adults. Refitting within each cohort on the shared 35-population atlas reduced prediction error by 6.2% in children (n = 147; P = .23) and 25.9% in adults (n = 113; P < .001), with wide intervals in each cohort; gains by baseline-titer quartile within each cohort are in supplemental Figure S15.

The score pointed the same way in both cohorts, more strongly in adults. The odds ratio per SD was 1.8 in children and 3.1 in adults (interaction P = .10), the rank correlation with the observed fold-rise was 0.37 (P = .002) and 0.50 (P ≤ .001), and fourfold rises were least frequent in the lowest fifth in both (22% and 10%; Figure 5B and C).

The predictive populations were also shared. Of the 10 populations that the pooled model selected in at least half of its bootstrap refits, 8 had the same direction of association in children and in adults, and the three with the largest pooled coefficients, CD21-low B cells (cluster 4), CCR4 + central-memory CD8 + T cells (cluster 8) and activated double-negative T cells (cluster 32), kept their direction in both (supplemental Table S6, Figure S16). How often a population was selected differed more than its direction: the adult refits leaned on CD21-low B cells, exhausted CD8 + T cells and CCR4 + central-memory CD8 + T cells, the pediatric refits on activated double-negative T cells. A model trained in children predicted adult responses well, while the reverse did not hold (supplemental Section S2.10, Figure S17).

3.8

The score predicted response a season later in the same recipients

The 74 second-season episodes of recipients vaccinated in consecutive years (35 adults and 39 children) were never used to build an atlas or fit a model, so they test the first-season models in the same recipients one season later (Figure 6). Adding immune-cell composition to baseline titers reduced the prediction error of the composite fold-rise in these episodes by 39.2% (95% recipient-bootstrap CI, 28.3-48.9%), and the AUC for a fourfold composite rise rose from 0.65 with titers alone to 0.71 with composition (difference, 0.06; 95% CI, -0.03 to 0.14; DeLong P = .20;

Figure 6A). Each SD of the first-season-standardized score was associated with 1.8-fold higher odds of a fourfold rise (95% CI, 0.9-3.5; P = .086).

Responses were stronger in the second season. After the first dose alone, 57% of returning recipients (39 of 69) had a fourfold composite rise in the second season, against 23% (16 of 69) of the same recipients and 23% (60 of 259) of all recipients in the first season (Figure 6B), and 50 of 74 second-season episodes met the fourfold criterion after 2 doses. Of the 72 recipients with both seasons, 22 changed from non-responder to responder, 6 from responder to non-responder, 16 responded in neither season and 28 in both, where a responder had at least a fourfold composite rise. Cellular scores rose from the first to the second season in every transition group (median increase, 0.1 to 0.9 SD), consistent with continuing immune reconstitution. On the response map of Figure 4C, recipients who became responders moved toward lower baseline titers and higher scores, and their median modeled probability of a fourfold rise rose from 0.31 to 0.68, whereas recipients who stopped responding moved toward higher titers, and their median modeled probability fell from 0.75 to 0.18 (Figure 6C). The titer trajectories explain the second movement (Figure 6D). A strong first-season response left high antibody at the start of the second season, which leaves little room for a further fourfold rise, while recipients who had not responded started the second season with low titers. Changes in response status between seasons therefore reflect both the baseline titer and the cellular score, as the first-season model predicts.

3.9 Fewer populations and markers carried the predictive information

A simpler assay would be more practical clinically, prompting us to test whether fewer populations and markers preserved prediction accuracy. On the shared atlas, we ranked populations inside each training fold and refitted the model with only the top-ranked ones. Five populations recovered most of the gain of all 35 (17.3% versus 18.1% beyond titers; supplemental Figure S18); CD21-low B cells and CCR4 + central-memory CD8 + T cells ranked in the top 5 in every fold, and activated double-negative T cells and cycling monocytes in 4 of 5. An unweighted signature of the 3 most positive and 3 most negative populations, chosen inside each training fold, gave a gain of the same size as the full composition (18.6%; 95% CI, 9.1-27.0%; supplemental Section S2.6, Table S7), so a handful of populations, counted and combined without weights, carries most of the signal.

Starting from the populations that drive the score, greedy forward selection, 40 which adds at each step the marker that most improves identification of these populations in training recipients, found that 8 markers (CD25, CD45RO, CD8, CD27, CD38, CCR4, CD19 and CXCR3) reproduced the 30marker cellular score computed from the same sampled cells of held-out recipients (r = 0.87; 95% CI, 0.80-0.93), although they assigned only 44% of cells to the correct population (supplemental Figure S19).

To reduce markers, for each training fold, we fixed the full-panel micro-centroids and their mapping to meta-clusters, including the number of clusters. Markers were prioritized by how strongly their expression differed across these meta-clusters. Using only the retained markers, we reassigned cells to the nearest micro-centroid and its meta-cluster. We then refitted M4 coefficients and penalty strength for each reduced panel; the prediction model itself was not held fixed.

Prediction error changed little across the tested panels. Test root mean squared error (RMSE) of the composite response was 1.395 with 30 markers, 1.403 with 15, 1.410 with eight and 1.380 with six, against 1.509 for baseline titers alone; fewer than six were not tested. Agreement with full-panel cell labels, by contrast, fell steadily, to 52.4% at 15 markers and 28.0% at six (Figure 7; supplemental Figure S20).

Fewer markers therefore preserved the predictive information more faithfully than they preserved individual cell labels. Scores computed from 15 markers tracked 30-marker scores computed from the same cells closely (r = 0.91 for the composite score, after removing differences between cross-validation folds), and the score-group differences in observed responses were somewhat smaller but in the same direction (supplemental Section S2.6, Figure S21).

The consensus 15-marker set is shown in Figure 7C and listed in supplemental Section S4.

3.10 The gain persisted in sensitivity analyses

The gain did not depend on the choices we made in building the models. Modeling the 3 antigens separately instead of their composite gave 11.2% beyond titers (supplemental Section S2.5). Running the whole pipeline on all 334 episodes with complete titers, from 262 recipients, with both seasons of a returning recipient kept in the same fold, gave 13.9% for the 3 antigens modeled jointly (P < .001; supplemental Section S3.7, Figures S22 and S23). Fixing the number of populations at any value between 5 and 42 instead of selecting it inside each fold gave between 10.4% and 16.4% (supplemental Section S1.2, Figure S24), so the result does not rest on how finely the cells were grouped. Allowing all pairwise interactions among titers and clinical factors did not change the picture: that model improved on titers alone by 3.1% without cellular composition and 15.7% with it (supplemental Table S8).

4 Discussion

In 260 allogeneic HCT recipients from 2 multicenter phase 2 randomized trials, the composition of peripheral blood before vaccination predicted the influenza antibody response beyond the pre-vaccination titer and the clinical factors a transplant physician already has. The practical outcome is the ability to identify the recipients least likely to respond to vaccination: in the lowest fifth of the cellular composition score, 15% reached a fourfold rise, compared with 51% of everyone else.

The same score separated responders from non-responders in children and in adults, with the main populations pointing in the same direction, and it held when the first-season models were applied to the held-out second-season vaccination episodes of the same recipients.

Read as a biomarker, a low score marks a recipient whose B-cell compartment has not returned. A blood composition compatible with a response appears to be necessary but not sufficient: recipients in the highest fifth of the score still failed to reach a fourfold rise 33% of the time, while in the lowest fifth only 15% responded at all. That asymmetry is what makes the score useful for identifying likely non-responders, and it is also why the score should not be read as a prediction that an individual recipient will respond.

These results extend the pediatric association evidence of Amarin and colleagues 27 by quantifying prediction beyond baseline titers and clinical factors in both cohorts, by turning the associations into one number per recipient, and by testing that number a season later in the same people.

Immune-cell composition reduced prediction error by 15.3% beyond titers alone and by 12.5% beyond titers plus clinical factors. A reduction in squared error is not a quantity clinicians work with, so we also put the signal on the antibody scale. At the same baseline titer, recipients at the 75th percentile of the score had post-vaccination titers about 2.2 times those at the 25th percentile (supplemental Table S9). For scale, 2 high-dose vaccinations produced adjusted geometric mean HAI titers about 1.2 to 2.1 times those after 2 standard doses in the adult trial, 10 and about 1.9 to 3.2 times in children vaccinated six or more months after transplantation. 11 The comparison is descriptive: vaccine dose was randomized, whereas the score summarizes observed differences between recipients. Against an assumed level of HAI assay noise, the gain from composition closes about 19% of the gap between the titers-only model and the smallest error the assay allows (supplemental Section S5, Table S10).

That difference matters most where recipients start low. A rise from 1:5 to 1:20 and a rise from 1:20 to 1:80 are both fourfold, but only the second leaves a titer in the range associated with protection in healthy adults, 35, 36 and the recipients who start at the detection limit are the ones in whom an extra doubling changes the end point. At high baseline titers the fold-rise is compressed by the ceiling of the assay and by the antibody already present, which is why we report the gain within baseline-titer quartiles rather than pooling across them.

What a transplant physician lacks before vaccinating is any way to tell, at the visit, which recipients are unlikely to seroconvert. Time since transplantation is the usual guide, and it is coarse: the score remained associated with the response after adjusting for it. A measurement taken from the same blood draw that identifies a fifth of recipients among whom fewer than one in five responds would, if it holds prospectively, be a reason to prioritize them for a second dose or a high-dose formulation, to revaccinate later, to give pre-exposure oseltamivir prophylaxis during the season, 41 or to protect them by vaccinating household contacts. 42 Each of those uses needs a prospective study with a defined threshold; what this study supports is that the measurement carries the information, not that a particular action follows from it. Models fitted separately in the high-dose and standard-dose arms ranked recipients similarly (Spearman ρ = 0.74; supplemental Figure S25), so these data do not identify who would gain from the high-dose formulation. The reduced panels point the same way: a flow-cytometry assay built on 15 or fewer markers would be the practical form of such a test. 43 The populations the model used read as a picture of where a recipient sits in immune reconstitution. B cells and activated T cells carry the positive side: the largest coefficients belong to CD21-low B cells (cluster 4) and activated double-negative T cells (cluster 32), and across the score range the B-cell share rises nearly eightfold from the lowest to the highest fifth, in line with reports linking B-cell recovery to vaccine responses after allogeneic transplantation. 20 CD21-low B cells are described as hyporesponsive in other settings, 44,45 so their positive association here needs biological characterization; one possibility is that in this setting they mark an expanding B-cell compartment rather than a functional subset.

The negative side is carried by cycling monocytes, activated lineage-negative cells, memory CD8 + T cells and plasmablasts (clusters 17, 29, 8, 1 and 28). Because composition is relative, a high share of one population implies a lower share of others, and the lowest-score recipients had more monocytes and lineage-negative cells and few B cells. One reading, which these data support but do not establish, is a staged recovery: myeloid and lineage-negative cells predominate early after transplantation, and the lymphoid compartment, especially B cells, fills in later (supplemental Figure S26). 3, 15 Time since transplantation is consistent with that reading without accounting for it: the composition features explained half the variance in that interval, and the score remained associated with response after adjustment for it.

Our populations differ from those Amarin and colleagues reported, 27 The score was derived and tested in the same two trials. Nested cross-validation and the held-out second season guard against overfitting within this population, but there is no external cohort, and the transfer analysis shows that a model trained in one cohort does not automatically carry to the other. Although the trials were multi-center and randomized, 10, 11 the recipients are those two protocols enrolled, and results may not generalize to other transplant populations or vaccination schedules. With 260 recipients, within-cohort and quartile comparisons are imprecise.

The models used baseline titers, age, sex, vaccine dose and time since transplantation. They did not use transplant type, conditioning intensity, donor source, graft-versus-host disease or ongoing immunosuppression, each of which shapes immune recovery and may itself be reflected in the cellular composition the model reads. How well the score holds across that heterogeneity is an open question that a larger or prospective cohort would answer.

Mass cytometry is not widely available clinically, so translation would require adaptation to spectral flow cytometry. 48 The panel lacks dedicated myeloid and NK-cell markers (CD14, CD16, CD11c, CD56) and CD45RA, so APC, monocyte and NK-like annotations were inferred by exclusion and EMRA T cells were defined without CD45RA; these labels should be validated with lineage-specific panels. Receiver operating characteristic curves for a fourfold composite rise in second-season episodes, from models with baseline titers alone or with immune-cell composition added. The error reduction is the bootstrap reduction in mean squared prediction error for the composite fold-rise. B. Percentage with a fourfold composite rise 4 weeks after the first dose, with 95% Wilson intervals, among all first-season recipients and among the 69 returning recipients with a one-dose titer in both seasons; the two dark bars are the same recipients. C. Each returning recipient's move from the first to the second season on the response map of Figure 4C, by response transition. Shading and white contours give the modeled probability of a fourfold rise from the first-season logistic regression on baseline titer and score. Gray arrows are individual recipients, the red arrow joins the group's median baseline titer and score from the first to the second season, and the red label gives the group's median modeled probability of a fourfold rise in the two seasons (not a change in score or in the share of responders). Second-season scores are standardized with the first season's fold mean and SD. D. Median HAI titer (geometric mean across antigens) before vaccination and 4 weeks after the second dose in each season, by response transition; the dashed line marks 1:40. Titers fell between seasons in every group. Because response is defined by the rise within a season, each group's status follows the slope of its segments: recipients who did not respond in either season stayed nearly flat, those who became responders started the second season low and rose, and those who stopped responding started the second season high after a strong first-season response.

Five were associated with a larger antibody response: B cells with low CD21 expression 234 (CD21-low; cluster 4), activated double-negative T cells with high CD38 and TIGIT expression 235 (cluster 32), naive CD8 + T cells (cluster 27), CCR7 -B cells (cluster 26) and senescent CD4 + T cells 236 (CD57 + ; cluster 33). Five were associated with a smaller response: central-memory CD8 + T cells 237 expressing CCR4 (cluster 8), cycling monocytes (Ki-67 + ; cluster 17), activated lineage-negative 238 cells expressing CD38, CD138 and CXCR3 (cluster 29), exhausted effector-memory CD8 + T cells (PD-1 + TIGIT + ; cluster 1) and plasmablasts (cluster 28). Population 8 is heterogeneous: about half of its cells are CD8 + CD4 -, a fifth CD4 + CD8 -and a quarter CD4 -CD8 -, with dim CD8 and high CD69, HLA-DR, CXCR3 and CCR4, so it is best read as an activated T-cell population rather than a single CD8 + subset. Baseline-titer terms remain among the largest coefficients; the cellular coefficients describe associations conditional on those titers and the clinical factors.

who found naive B cells, naive CD4 + T cells and circulating T follicular helper cells as univariate predictors in the pediatric cohort. Two differences are expected. Their analysis tested populations one at a time, which favors the populations positively associated with response, whereas a penalized model fitted to all populations at once keeps whichever combination predicts best and can drop a strong univariate predictor whose information another population already supplies. And a compositional model gives weight to populations that are high when the response is poor, which is how the monocyte and lineage-negative populations enter here. Circulating T follicular helper cells, which have forecast influenza responses in other studies, 46,47 were not resolved as a separate population at the resolution of our atlas, and the CD4 + population richest in PD-1, ICOS and CXCR5 (cluster 25) contributed little to this model. Prediction did not need the full panel. An unweighted signature of six populations predicted as well as all 35, and both the 15-marker and the smaller top-down panels reproduced most of the score. That a simpler count carries the same information argues that most of the signal lies in the broad composition, and it is what makes a flow-cytometry version of this measurement plausible. Reproducing a score is not the same as reproducing a cell-type assignment: at 15 markers only about half of cells kept their full-panel label.

A reduced panel on spectral flow cytometry would need its own atlas, clustered and calibrated on those markers, and combining the cellular measurement with other baseline markers, such as gene expression or serum cytokines, may add what composition alone does not.
