Figure 1 :

Figure1: Study design and pre-vaccination immune-cell populations. A. The 260 first-season recipients came from two parallel randomized trials of two doses of high-dose versus standard-dose inactivated influenza vaccine, one pediatric and one adult. Ages are medians with ranges, and vaccination occurred 3 to 36 months after transplantation (median 6). Seventy-four second-season episodes of recipients who re-enrolled the following season were held out for validation. At V1 the first dose was given and pre-vaccination blood was profiled by 30-marker mass cytometry; that profile and the baseline HAI titers are the model inputs. The outcome is the mean log fold-rise in HAI titer, log(V3/V1), across H1N1, H3N2 and B/Victoria, where V3 is 4 weeks after the second dose. Titers at the second-dose visit (V2) are used only in the second-season comparison, those at a later follow-up (V4) not at all, and B/Yamagata was excluded because it is not in the high-dose formulation for adults. Visit spacing is schematic. B. Each point is a cell, placed by UMAP according to its 30-marker cytometry profile. Colors indicate the annotated lineage and numbers identify the 35 populations of the shared atlas built from both cohorts. The models use each recipient's share of cells in each population. APC, monocyte and NK-like labels are provisional because the panel lacks dedicated myeloid and NK-cell markers. DNT, double-negative T cells.

Figure 2 :

Figure2: Added predictive value of immune-cell composition. Points show the percentage reduction in mean squared error of the predicted composite antibody response between models; positive values favor the second model. Navy marks the two comparisons that isolate immune-cell composition. Models contain baseline titers (M1), titers and clinical factors (M2), titers and immune-cell composition (M3), or all three (M4). Predictions were evaluated in recipients excluded from model fitting, with atlas construction and tuning performed inside training folds. Whiskers are 95% intervals from 2,000 paired bootstrap samples that resample whole recipients. All comparisons use the same 260 recipients.

Figure 3 :

Figure 3: Predictors of the composite antibody response. Bars show standardized coefficients of the model containing baseline titers, clinical factors and immune-cell composition (M4), fitted on the shared 35population atlas in 200 bootstrap resamples of recipients: the coefficient averaged across refits, multiplied by the predictor's standard deviation. A. Baseline titers and clinical factors. B. The ten immune-cell populations selected in at least half of the refits (CM, central memory; DNT, double-negative T cells; EM, effector memory; Mono, monocytes). Positive values indicate a higher predicted composite log fold-rise and negative values a lower one, accounting for the other predictors. A negative baseline-titer coefficient means that a higher starting titer predicts a smaller proportional rise, not a lower post-vaccination titer. Horizontal scales differ between A and B.

Figure 4 :

Figure 4: The cellular composition score in clinical terms. Scores come from models fitted without the recipient and were standardized within cross-validation fold. A. Median share of PBMCs in each cell lineage among recipients in the lowest (orange) and highest (navy) fifth of the score; numbers give the fold difference, colored by the group with the larger share. B. Percentage of recipients with a fourfold composite rise (geometric mean fold-rise across the 3 antigens of at least four) in each fifth of the score, with 95% Wilson intervals; the dashed line marks the overall rate. C. Baseline titer and score of each recipient. Open circles mark recipients below a fourfold rise; translucent blue circles mark a fourfold rise or more, with area increasing with the composite fold-rise, so overlapping points remain visible. Shading and contours show the modeled probability of a fourfold rise from a logistic regression on baseline titer and score. D. Receiver operating characteristic curves for a fourfold composite rise from out-of-fold predictions of the model with baseline titers alone (M1) and the model that adds immune-cell composition (M3); the P value comes from the null bootstrap that repeats the whole model fitting.

Figure 5 :

Figure 5: Children and adults share the cellular profile. A. Reduction in mean squared error of the predicted composite response when immune-cell composition is added to baseline titers (M1→M3) or to titers and clinical factors (M2→M4), for the pooled model evaluated within each cohort (dark bars) and for models refitted within each cohort (light bars), in 147 pediatric and 113 adult recipients. Whiskers are 95% recipient-bootstrap intervals. The cohort-specific models used the shared atlas built from both cohorts and are exploratory. B. Percentage of recipients with a fourfold composite rise by fifth of the score (fifths defined on all recipients), with 95% Wilson intervals; the interaction P value tests whether the association of the score with the response differs between cohorts. C. Odds ratio of a fourfold composite rise per SD of the score, adjusted for baseline titer, with 95% intervals and Wald P values.

Figure 6 :

Figure6: Validation in the second season of the same recipients. First-season models scored the 74 second-season episodes with recorded titers. These episodes were used neither to build an atlas nor to fit a model, and each was scored by the model that held out its recipient. A responder had at least a fourfold composite rise. A. Receiver operating characteristic curves for a fourfold composite rise in second-season episodes, from models with baseline titers alone or with immune-cell composition added. The error reduction is the bootstrap reduction in mean squared prediction error for the composite fold-rise. B. Percentage with a fourfold composite rise 4 weeks after the first dose, with 95% Wilson intervals, among all first-season recipients and among the 69 returning recipients with a one-dose titer in both seasons; the two dark bars are the same recipients. C. Each returning recipient's move from the first to the second season on the response map of Figure4C, by response transition. Shading and white contours give the modeled probability of a fourfold rise from the first-season logistic regression on baseline titer and score. Gray arrows are individual recipients, the red arrow joins the group's median baseline titer and score from the first to the second season, and the red label gives the group's median modeled probability of a fourfold rise in the two seasons (not a change in score or in the share of responders). Second-season scores are standardized with the first season's fold mean and SD. D. Median HAI titer (geometric mean across antigens) before vaccination and 4 weeks after the second dose in each season, by response transition; the dashed line marks 1:40. Titers fell between seasons in every group. Because response is defined by the rise within a season, each group's status follows the slope of its segments: recipients who did not respond in either season stayed nearly flat, those who became responders started the second season low and rose, and those who stopped responding started the second season high after a strong first-season response.

Figure 7 :

Figure 7: Prediction with fewer cytometry markers. The full-panel atlas was held fixed while cells were reassigned using smaller marker sets; prediction models were refitted for each set. A. Percentage of cells retaining their full-panel meta-cluster label, shown as the mean and standard deviation across 5 folds. B. Held-out root mean squared prediction error of the composite response for the model containing titers, clinical factors, and immune-cell composition (M4); lower values indicate better prediction. The dotted horizontal line shows the baseline-titer-only model. C. The 15 markers with the largest average between-meta-cluster variance across folds; individual fold-specific panels could differ. Prediction used up to 2,000 cells per episode at every marker budget, so the full-panel reference differs from the full-depth primary analysis. The vertical dashed line marks 15 markers.

Table 1 . 192 Table 1 :

Characteristics of the 260 recipients.Values are median (range), n (%), or as labeled. Months since transplantation and the baseline titer are measured at the vaccination visit; the baseline titer is the geometric mean HAI titer across H1N1, H3N2 and B/Victoria. A fourfold composite rise is a geometric mean fold-rise of at least 4 from the day of vaccination to 4 weeks after the second dose.

Table 2 :

Four-model ladder: prediction of the composite antibody response. A. Mean squared error (MSE) of the out-of-fold predicted composite log fold-rise, from 5-fold nested cross-validation; lower is better. All models are elastic nets (α = 0.9). B. Pairwise comparisons. ∆MSE% is the relative reduction in MSE, so positive values mean the richer model predicts better. Intervals are paired bootstrap (B = 2,000) resampling whole recipients; P is two-sided.

Table 3 :

Prediction by baseline-titer quartile. Mean squared error of the out-of-fold predicted composite log fold-rise for the model with baseline titers (M1) and the model that adds immune-cell composition (M3), within quartiles of the geometric mean baseline titer across the three antigens. The interval is a recipient-level bootstrap (B = 2,000).
